h=\frac{1}{1-t}(\Phi_{t}^{-1})^{*}\acute{G}-\overline{g}
remains small in a
C^{2}
sense.
Informally, these well-posedness results imply that, at the first time
\mathbf{p}
exits the set
\mathcal{P}
(given by Definition
4.22
), it must be because
h_{\mathbf{p}}
fails to satisfy the estimates defining
\mathcal{B}
and not because the harmonic map heat flow solutions
\tilde{\Phi}
fail to exist nor because we’ve reached the singular time of
G_{\mathbf{p}}
.

Lemma 8.3
.

If
\overline{p}\leq 1
,

\Gamma_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f
),

0<\epsilon_{1}\ll 1
is sufficiently small (depending on
n
),

0<\epsilon_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}
),

0<\gamma_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\epsilon_{0}
),
and
\tau_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\Gamma_{0},\gamma_{0}
),
then,
for all
|\mathbf{p}|\leq\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}
,
there exists
t_{1}=t_{1}(\mathbf{p})>t_{0}
such that

\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}[\lambda_{*},\overline{p},\Gamma_{0},\gamma_{0},\mu_{u}=2,\mu_{s}=2,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t_{0},t_{1}].

In particular, for all
|\mathbf{p}|\leq\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}
,

t_{1}^{*}=t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})\doteqdot\sup\{t_{1}\in(t_{0},1]:\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}[\lambda_{*},\overline{p},\Gamma_{0},\gamma_{0},\mu_{u}=2,\mu_{s}=2,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t_{0},t_{1}]\}

is well-defined and
t_{1}^{*}>t_{0}
.

Proof.

First note that
T(\mathbf{p})>t_{0}
follows automatically from the short-time existence of the Ricci flow
G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
.

The existence of a harmonic map heat flow solution
\tilde{\Phi}
will follow from Proposition
B.1
after we confirm that
\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
and
(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}
satisfy the assumptions of the proposition.
Since
G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
has bounded curvature for short time,

G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
remains in an arbitrarily small
C^{12}(M)
-neighborhood of
G_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{0})
for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
if
0<t_{1}-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small.
Similarly,
(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}
remains in an arbitrarily small
C^{12}(M)
-neighborhood of
(1-t_{0})\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}
for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
if
0<t_{1}-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small.
Combined with the derivative bounds for
\eta_{\Gamma_{0}}
, it follows that

\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\eta_{\Gamma_{0}}\iota_{*}G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)+(1-\eta_{\Gamma_{0}})(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}

remains in an arbitrarily small
C^{12}(M)
-neighborhood of
\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{0})

for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
if
0<t_{1}-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small.
Thus, the derivatives of order at most
10
of the curvature of
\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)

are uniformly bounded for
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
if
0<t_{1}-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small.
When
(1-t_{0})\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\Gamma_{0}
, Lemma
5.4
implies uniform bounds on

\left|\partial_{t}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)+2Rc[\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)]\right|_{\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)}

for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]\subset[t_{0},T(\mathbf{p}))
.
Finally,
\tilde{\Phi}_{t_{0}}=Id_{M}
is a diffeomorphism and

\displaystyle\left|\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{0})-(1-t_{0})\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}\right|_{(1-t_{0})\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}}=\left|\eta_{\gamma_{0}}\sum_{j=1}^{K}p_{j}h_{j}\right|_{\overline{g}}\lesssim_{n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*}}\overline{p}\gamma_{0}^{|\lambda_{*}|}

by the proof of Proposition
4.7
.
When additionally
\overline{p}\leq 1
and
\gamma_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\epsilon_{0}
,
this estimate implies

\left|\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{0})-(1-t_{0})\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}\right|_{(1-t_{0})\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{0}.

If
\epsilon_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n
, then Proposition
B.1
applies to give the short-time existence of a smooth family of diffeomorphisms
\tilde{\Phi}:M\times[t_{0},t_{1})\to M
solving the harmonic map heat flow with initial condition
\tilde{\Phi}_{t_{0}}=Id_{M}
.
Additionally, Proposition
B.1
states that this
\tilde{\Phi}
may be chosen such that

\left|\frac{1}{1-t}(\Phi_{t}^{-1})^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)-\overline{g}\right|_{\overline{g}}=\left|(\tilde{\Phi}_{t}^{-1})^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)-(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}\right|_{(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}}\leq\epsilon_{0}

on
M\times[t_{0},t_{1}).

Recall
\tau=-\ln(1-t),\tau_{0}=-\ln(1-t_{0})
, and denote
\tau_{1}=-\ln(1-t_{1})<\infty
.
By Corollary
4.17
,

h(\tau)=h_{\mathbf{p}}(t(\tau))
is a smooth solution of

(
6.1
)

\partial_{\tau}h=\overline{\Delta}_{f}h+2\overline{Rm}[h]+\mathcal{E}_{1}+\mathcal{E}_{2}\qquad\text{ on }M\times(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

where
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
are defined as in (
6.2
), (
6.3
) respectively.
By Lemma
8.2
, we may assume

\sum_{m=1}^{3}|\overline{\nabla}^{m}h(\tau_{0})|_{\overline{g}}\leq C(n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*})\overline{p}\gamma_{0}^{|\lambda_{*}|}\leq\epsilon_{0}

if
\overline{p}\leq 1
,

0<\gamma_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\epsilon_{0}
,
and
\tau_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\gamma_{0}
.
Since
|h(\tau)|_{\overline{g}}\leq\epsilon_{0}
for
\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
,
Lemma
6.9
with
\delta=\epsilon_{0}
therefore implies that

|\overline{\nabla}h|_{\overline{g}}(x,\tau)+|\overline{\nabla}^{2}h|_{\overline{g}}(x,\tau)\lesssim_{n,M,\overline{g},f}\epsilon_{0}\qquad\text{ on }\{(x,\tau)\in M\times[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}):4\gamma_{0}e^{\tau}<f(x)\}

if also
\epsilon_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f
.
Since
h
is smooth and the complement of
\{4\gamma_{0}e^{\tau_{1}}<f\}\subset M
is compact, it follows that, after possibly taking
\tau_{1}
smaller, in fact

|\overline{\nabla}h|_{\overline{g}}+|\overline{\nabla}^{2}h|_{\overline{g}}\lesssim_{n,M,\overline{g},f}\epsilon_{0}\qquad\text{ on }M\times[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}).

In particular,

\displaystyle|\overline{\nabla}h|_{\overline{g}}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{1}\text{ on }M\times[\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\text{ and }

\displaystyle|\overline{\nabla}^{2}h|_{\overline{g}}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{2}\text{ on }M\times[\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

if
0<\epsilon_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}
.

With these
C^{2}
estimates for
h
, the proof of Lemma
5.5
now shows that

\supp h(\tau)\subset\{f\leq\Gamma_{0}e^{\tau}\}\qquad\text{ for all }\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

so long as
\Gamma_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f
,

0<\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1}\ll 1
are sufficiently small depending on
n
,
and
\tau_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\Gamma_{0}
.
In particular,
h(\tau)
is compactly supported for all
\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.

Finally, Lemma
8.1
ensures that, at
\tau_{0}
,

\displaystyle\|\pi_{u}h_{\mathbf{p}}(\tau_{0})\|_{L^{2}_{f}}\leq\frac{3}{2}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\|\pi_{s}h_{\mathbf{p}}(\tau_{0})\|_{L^{2}_{f}}\leq\frac{3}{2}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}

if
\overline{p}\leq 1
and
\tau_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\gamma_{0}
.
Since
h_{\mathbf{p}}
is smooth and supported in
\{f\leq\Gamma_{0}e^{\tau_{1}}\}
for all
\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
,
these
L^{2}_{f}
quantities are continuous in
\tau
.
Therefore, after possibly taking
t_{1}-t_{0}
or equivalently
\tau_{1}-\tau_{0}
smaller, it follows that
for all
\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
,

\displaystyle\|\pi_{u}h_{\mathbf{p}}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}_{f}}\leq 2e^{\lambda_{*}\tau}\quad\text{ and }\quad\|\pi_{s}h_{\mathbf{p}}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}_{f}}\leq 2e^{\lambda_{*}\tau}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.4
.

By Definition
4.22
of
\mathcal{P}
,
t_{1}<t_{1}^{\prime}
implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}[\lambda_{*},\overline{p},\Gamma_{0},\gamma_{0},\mu_{u},\mu_{s},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t_{0},t_{1}^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\subset{}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}[\lambda_{*},\overline{p},\Gamma_{0},\gamma_{0},\mu_{u},\mu_{s},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t_{0},t_{1}].

Thus,
t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
may also be written as

t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})\\
=\inf(\{t_{1}\in(t_{0},1]:\mathbf{p}\notin\mathcal{P}[\lambda_{*},\overline{p},\Gamma_{0},\gamma_{0},\mu_{u}=2,\mu_{s}=2,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t_{0},t_{1}]\}\cup\{1\}).

Lemma 8.5
.

Assume the constants
\overline{p},\Gamma_{0},\gamma_{0},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\tau_{0}
are taken sufficiently large or small as in the assumptions of Lemma
8.3
so that
t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
is well-defined.
Additionally, assume that

0<\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\ll 1
are sufficiently small (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f
).

If
t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})<1
, then

t_{1}^{*}<T(\mathbf{p})
and
there exists
t_{1}^{+}\in(t_{1}^{*},T)
such that
there exists a unique smooth solution
\tilde{\Phi}:M\times[t_{0},t_{1}^{+})\to M
to the harmonic map heat flow
satisfying

(1)

\tilde{\Phi}_{t}=\tilde{\Phi}(\cdot,t):M\to M
is a diffeomorphism for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}^{+})
, and

(2)

with
\Phi_{t}=\varphi_{t}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{t}
,

h_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\frac{1}{1-t}(\Phi_{t}^{-1})^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)-\overline{g}\in\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u}=4,\mu_{s}=4,2\epsilon_{0},2\epsilon_{1},2\epsilon_{2},[t_{0},t_{1}^{+})]

where
\mathcal{B}
is defined as in Definition
4.21
.

Moreover,
for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}^{+})
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{t}(\overline{\Omega_{\eta_{\Gamma_{0}}}})\subset{}

\displaystyle\{\Gamma_{0}/2<f<\Gamma_{0}\},

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}(\overline{\Omega_{\eta_{\Gamma_{0}}}})\subset{}

\displaystyle\{(1-t)^{-1}<f<\Gamma_{0}(1-t)^{-1}\},\text{ and}

\displaystyle h_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=0\,\text{ on }

\displaystyle\{f\geq\Gamma_{0}(1-t)^{-1}\}

where recall
\Omega_{\eta_{\Gamma_{0}}}=\{x\in M:0<\eta_{\Gamma_{0}}(x)<1\}
as in Subsection
5.1
.

Proof.

To simplify the notation throughout this proof, we shall write

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u}=2,\mu_{s}=2,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},I]\quad\text{as}\quad\mathcal{B}[\mu_{u,s}=2,\epsilon_{0,1,2},I].

We first establish curvature estimates for
G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
and
\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
.
Let
t_{1}\in[t_{0},t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
.
It follows from the definition of
t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
and
\mathcal{P}
(Definition
4.22
) that
there exists
\tilde{\Phi}:M\times[t_{0},t_{1})\to M
which is a smooth solution to the harmonic map heat flow (
4.7
)
such that
\tilde{\Phi}_{t}:M\to M
is a diffeomorphism for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1})
and

h_{\mathbf{p}}\in\mathcal{B}[\mu_{u,s}=2,\epsilon_{0,1,2},[t_{0},t_{1})]
.

Recall from the Definition
4.22
of
\mathcal{P}
that
h(t)=h_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
therefore satisfies the estimates

|\overline{\nabla}^{l}h(t)|_{\overline{g}}\leq\epsilon_{l}\qquad\text{for all }t\in[t_{0},t_{1}),0\leq l\leq 2.

Hence, if
\epsilon_{0}+\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g}
,
then
g_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\overline{g}+h_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
is a family of complete metrics
such that

\sup_{M\times[t_{0},t_{1})}|Rm_{g_{\mathbf{p}}(t)}|_{g_{\mathbf{p}}(t)}\leq 2\sup_{M}|\overline{Rm}|_{\overline{g}}.

Since
t_{1}<t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})<1
and the
\tilde{\Phi}_{t}
are diffeomorphisms,

\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=(1-t)(\varphi_{t}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{t})^{*}g_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
will also be a family of complete metrics with curvature bounds

(8.6)

\sup_{M\times[t_{0},t_{1})}|Rm_{\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)}|_{\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)}\leq 2\sup_{M\times[t_{0},t_{1})}\frac{1}{1-t}|\overline{Rm}|_{\overline{g}}=\frac{2}{1-t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})}\sup_{M}|\overline{Rm}|_{\overline{g}}<\infty.

Let
U\subset M
be the interior of the set where
\eta_{\Gamma_{0}}=1
and note that
\overline{\{f<\Gamma_{0}/2\}}\subset U
by construction of
\eta_{\Gamma_{0}}
.
By construction,
\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\iota_{*}G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
on
U\times[t_{0},t_{1})
.
From the curvature bounds (
8.6
) combined with the pseudolocality Proposition
5.2
,
it follows that there exists
\mathcal{K}_{0}
depending only on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\Gamma_{0},t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
such that

\sup_{\mathcal{M}\times[t_{0},t_{1})}|Rm_{G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)}|_{G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)}\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}<\infty

as long as

\Gamma_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f
and

0<1-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\Gamma_{0}
.
Because
G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
is a closed Ricci flow solution,
these curvature bounds imply the Ricci flow solution
G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
exists for slightly larger times.
In other words,
T(\mathbf{p})>t_{1}

and, by continuity,

\sup_{\mathcal{M}\times[t_{0},t_{1}]}|Rm_{G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)}|_{G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)}\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}.

Note that
\mathcal{K}_{0}
depends on
t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
but not
t_{1}
.
Since
t_{1}<t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
was arbitrary and
G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
is a closed Ricci flow solution,
it follows that

\sup_{\mathcal{M}\times[t_{0},t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})]}|Rm_{G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)}|_{G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)}\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}

and thus
t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})<T(\mathbf{p})
.

Derivative estimates for Ricci flow then imply that there exist
constants
\mathcal{K}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{K}_{12}
(depending on
n
and
\mathcal{K}_{0}
)
and
T^{\prime}\in(t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),T(\mathbf{p}))
such that

\sup_{\mathcal{M}\times[t_{0},T^{\prime}]}|\nabla^{m}Rm_{G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)}|_{G_{\mathbf{p}}(t)}\leq 2\mathcal{K}_{m}

for all
0\leq m\leq 12
.
Taking
\mathcal{K}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{K}_{12}
possibly larger depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,t_{1}^{*}
and
T^{\prime}-t_{1}^{*}
possibly smaller, we may additionally assume the soliton also satisfies

\sup_{M\times[t_{0},T^{\prime}]}|\nabla^{m}Rm_{(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}}|_{(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}}\leq 2\mathcal{K}_{m}

for all
0\leq m\leq 12
.
If
0<1-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\Gamma_{0}
,
derivative estimates for
\eta_{\Gamma_{0}}
and the fact that

\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\eta_{\Gamma_{0}}\iota_{*}G+(1-\eta_{\Gamma_{0}})(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g},

then imply that the curvature of
\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
and its derivatives of order
\leq 10
are uniformly bounded on
M\times[t_{0},T^{\prime}]
.

Next, we establish the existence of the harmonic map heat flow solution
\tilde{\Phi}
on the time interval
[t_{0},t_{1}^{+})
.
Again, let
t_{1}<t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
and let

\tilde{\Phi}:M\times[t_{0},t_{1})\to M
be a smooth solution to the harmonic map heat flow
such that
\tilde{\Phi}_{t}:M\to M
is a diffeomorphism for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1})
and

h_{\mathbf{p}}\in\mathcal{B}[\mu_{u,s}=2,\epsilon_{0,1,2},[t_{0},t_{1})]
.
Let
t_{1}^{-}\in(t_{0},t_{1})
.
Note that

\sup_{M}\left|(\tilde{\Phi}_{t_{1}^{-}}^{-1})^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{1}^{-})-(1-t_{1}^{-})\varphi_{t_{1}^{-}}^{*}\overline{g}\right|_{(1-t_{1}^{-})\varphi_{t_{1}^{-}}^{*}\overline{g}}=\sup_{M}\left|h_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{1}^{-})\right|_{\overline{g}}\leq\epsilon_{0}.

By Lemma
5.4
and the fact that the curvature of
\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
has derivatives which are uniformly bounded up to order
10
on the time interval
[t_{1}^{-},T^{\prime}]\subset[t_{0},T^{\prime}]
,
the short-time existence Proposition
B.1
applies to yield a smooth family of diffeomorphisms
\tilde{\tilde{\Phi}}:M\times[t_{1}^{-},t_{1}^{-}+\delta]\to M
solving

\partial_{t}\tilde{\tilde{\Phi}}=\Delta_{\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t),(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}}\tilde{\tilde{\Phi}}\qquad\text{ with initial condition }\tilde{\tilde{\Phi}}_{t_{1}^{-}}=\tilde{\Phi}_{t_{1}^{-}}

so long as
\epsilon_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n
.
Moreover, Proposition
B.1
implies

\delta\in(0,T^{\prime}-t_{1}^{*}]
can be taken to be independent of
t_{1}^{-}
and
t_{1}

and such that

(8.7)

\left|\left(\tilde{\tilde{\Phi}}_{t}^{-1}\right)^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)-(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}\right|_{(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}}\leq 2\epsilon_{0}\qquad\text{ on }M\times[t_{1}^{-},t_{1}^{-}+\delta].

Now, fix
t_{1}^{-}
and
t_{1}
sufficiently close to
t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
so that
t_{1}^{-}+\delta>t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
and consider the map
\tilde{\tilde{\Phi}}
as above.
We shall use the uniqueness result for the harmonic map heat flow (Proposition
B.6
) to argue that in fact
\tilde{\tilde{\Phi}}=\tilde{\Phi}
on the intersection of their domains and thereby obtain the desired extension to the time interval
[t_{0},t_{1}^{+})
for some
t_{1}^{+}\in(t_{1}^{*},t_{1}^{-}+\delta)
.
Suppose for the sake of contradiction that

\tilde{\Phi}(x,t)\neq\tilde{\tilde{\Phi}}(x,t)\qquad\text{ for some }(x,t)\in M\times[t_{1}^{-},t_{1})

and consider

t_{2}^{*}=\inf\{t\in[t_{1}^{-},t_{1}):\text{there exists }x\in M\text{ such that }\tilde{\Phi}(x,t)\neq\tilde{\tilde{\Phi}}(x,t)\}\in[t_{1}^{-},t_{1}).

Since
T^{\prime}<1
,
(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}
has uniform curvature bounds and injectivity radius lower bounds on
M\times[t_{0},T^{\prime}]\supset[t_{1}^{-},t_{1}]
.
Let

\tilde{h}(t)=(\tilde{\Phi}_{t}^{-1})^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)-(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\tilde{h}}(t)=\left(\tilde{\tilde{\Phi}}_{t}^{-1}\right)^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)-(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}.

Then

\sup_{M\times[t_{0},t_{1})}\left|\tilde{h}(t)\right|_{(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}}\leq\epsilon_{0},\qquad\sup_{M\times[t_{1}^{-},t_{1}^{-}+\delta]}\left|\tilde{\tilde{h}}(t)\right|_{(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}}\leq 2\epsilon_{0},

\text{and }\sup_{M\times[t_{0},t_{1})}\left|{}^{(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}}\nabla\tilde{h}(t)\right|_{(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}}\leq\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{\sqrt{1-t}}.

By similar logic as in the proof of Lemma
8.3
, one obtains derivative estimates for
\tilde{\tilde{h}}
of the form

\left|{}^{(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}}\nabla\tilde{\tilde{h}}(t)\right|_{(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}}\lesssim_{n,M,\overline{g},f}\frac{\epsilon_{0}+\epsilon_{1}}{\sqrt{1-t}}

for all
t
in a neighborhood of
t_{2}^{*}

if
0<\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1}\ll 1
are sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f
and

1-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f
.
If
0<\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1}\ll 1
are sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f
,
then Proposition
B.6
implies that
\tilde{\Phi}(x,t)=\tilde{\tilde{\Phi}}(x,t)
for all
x\in M
and all
t
in a neighborhood of
t_{2}^{*}
, which contradicts the definition of
t_{2}^{*}
.
This contradiction implies
\tilde{\Phi}=\tilde{\tilde{\Phi}}
on the intersection of their domains, and we thereby obtain the desired extension to the time interval
[t_{0},t_{1}^{-}+\delta)\supsetneq[t_{0},t_{1}^{*})
by gluing.

Note in particular that

\displaystyle\sup_{M\times[t_{0},t_{1}^{-}+\delta)}\left|h_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\right|_{\overline{g}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{M\times[t_{0},t_{1}^{-}+\delta)}\left|\frac{1}{1-t}(\Phi_{t}^{-1})^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)-\overline{g}\right|_{\overline{g}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{M\times[t_{0},t_{1}^{-}+\delta)}\left|(\tilde{\Phi}_{t}^{-1})^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)-(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}\right|_{(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon_{0}

by equation (
8.7
).
Similar logic as in the proof of Lemma
8.3
then implies that in fact, for some
t_{1}^{+}\in(t_{1}^{*},t_{1}^{-}+\delta)
,

h_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\frac{1}{1-t}(\Phi_{t}^{-1})^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)-\overline{g}\in\mathcal{B}[\mu_{u,s}=4,2\epsilon_{0},2\epsilon_{1},2\epsilon_{2},[t_{0},t_{1}^{+})]

if the constants
\overline{p},\Gamma_{0},\gamma_{0},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\tau_{0}
are taken sufficiently large or small as in the assumptions of Lemma
8.3
.
This completes the proof of the existence statement for
\tilde{\Phi}
.

The uniqueness statement follows from Proposition
B.6
.
Finally, the last statement of the lemma is a direct consequence of Lemma
5.5
.
∎

8.3.
Completing the Proof of
1.1

In this subsection, we complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
through the use of a Ważewski box argument.

Assumption 8.6
.

Throughout this subsection or equivalently the remainder of the paper,
we assume that the constants
\overline{p},\Gamma_{0},\gamma_{0},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\tau_{0}

sufficiently large or small so that Lemmas
8.3
and
8.5
apply.
In other words,

0<\overline{p}\leq 1
,

\Gamma_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f
),

0<\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\ll 1
are sufficiently small (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f
),

0<\epsilon_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}
),

0<\gamma_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\epsilon_{0}
), and

\tau_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\Gamma_{0},\gamma_{0}
).

Also, throughout this subsection, suppose for the sake of contradiction that
t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})<1
for all
|\mathbf{p}|\leq\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}
.
We will arrive at a contradiction at the end of this subsection in the proof of
1.1
.

For each
\mathbf{p}
, consider the smooth family of diffeomorphisms
\tilde{\Phi}:M\times[t_{0},t_{1}^{+}(\mathbf{p}))\to M
as in Lemma
8.5
and let

h_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\frac{1}{1-t}(\Phi_{t}^{-1})^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)-\overline{g}\qquad\text{ for all }t\in[t_{0},t_{1}^{+}(\mathbf{p})).

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
|\mathbf{p}|\leq\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}
.
For all
0<\mu_{u},\mu_{s}<2
and all
t_{2}\in(t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),t_{1}^{+}(\mathbf{p}))
,

h_{\mathbf{p}}\notin\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u},\mu_{s},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},[t_{0},t_{2}]].

Proof.

Suppose not.
Then there exists
0<\mu_{u},\mu_{s}<2
and
t_{2}\in(t_{1}^{*},t_{1}^{+})

such that

\displaystyle h_{\mathbf{p}}

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u},\mu_{s},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},[t_{0},t_{2}]]

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},2,2,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},[t_{0},t_{2})].

Hence,

\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}[\lambda_{*},\overline{p},\Gamma_{0},\gamma_{0},2,2,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t_{0},t_{2}]

and
t_{2}>t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, which contradicts the definition of
t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
.
∎

For
0<\mu_{u},\mu_{s}\leq 2
, define

(8.8)

t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\inf\{t_{2}\in[t_{0},t_{1}^{+}(\mathbf{p})):h_{\mathbf{p}}\notin\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u},\mu_{s},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},[t_{0},t_{2}]]\}

as the first time
h_{\mathbf{p}}
exits the set
\mathcal{B}
defined with parameters
\mu_{u},\mu_{s}
.
By Lemma
8.7
,
t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
is well-defined
and

t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})\leq t_{2,2}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})<t_{1}^{+}(\mathbf{p})<1.

Moreover,

h_{\mathbf{p}}\in\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u},\mu_{s},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},[t_{0},t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})]].

Indeed, this follows from the fact that
h_{\mathbf{p}}
is smooth, its support can be controlled by Lemma
8.5
, and that Definition
4.21
of
\mathcal{B}
is defined by closed conditions.

For the closed ball
\overline{B_{\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{K}
of radius
\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}
centered at the origin,
we consider the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:\overline{B_{\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}}}\to\mathbb{R}^{K}\qquad\text{ given by}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{p})=\left(\big(h_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})),h_{1}\big)_{L^{2}_{f}(M)},\dots,\big(h_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})),h_{K}\big)_{L^{2}_{f}(M)}\right).

Lemma 8.8
.

Assume
0<\mu_{u}<\frac{1}{2}\overline{p}
and

\tau_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\gamma_{0},\mu_{u}
).
Consider the closed annulus
\overline{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{K}
given by

\overline{A}=\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}:2\mu_{u}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}\leq|\mathbf{p}|\leq\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}\}.

For all
\mathbf{p}\in\overline{A}
and any
0<\mu_{s}<2
,
t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=t_{0}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{F}\left(\overline{A}\right)\subset\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
and the restricted map

\mathcal{F}|_{\overline{A}}:\overline{A}\to\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}

is homotopic to the identity
Id:\overline{A}\to\overline{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

Let
2\mu_{u}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}\leq|\mathbf{p}|\leq\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}
.
Then

\displaystyle\|\pi_{u}h_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{0})\|_{L^{2}_{f}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{K}(h_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{0}),h_{j})_{L^{2}_{f}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq|\mathbf{p}|-C(n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*})e^{-\frac{\gamma_{0}}{100}e^{\tau_{0}}}

\displaystyle(\text{Lemma \ref{Lem L^2_f Ests at t_0}})

\displaystyle\geq 2\mu_{u}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}-C(n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*})e^{-\frac{\gamma_{0}}{100}e^{\tau_{0}}}

\displaystyle>\mu_{u}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}

if
\tau_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\gamma_{0},\mu_{u}
.

\|\pi_{u}h_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{0})\|_{L^{2}_{f}}>\mu_{u}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}

therefore implies

h_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{0})\notin\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u},\mu_{s},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\{t_{0}\}]

and thus
t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=t_{0}
.

Now, consider the straight-line homotopy from the identity to
\mathcal{F}
, that is

\mathcal{F}_{s}(\mathbf{p})\doteqdot\mathbf{p}+s(\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{p})-\mathbf{p}),\qquad(s\in[0,1]).

If
\tau_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\gamma_{0},\mu_{u}
, then
for any
\mathbf{p}\in\overline{A}
and
s\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\quad\,\left|\mathbf{p}+s(\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{p})-\mathbf{p})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbf{p}+s\left(\big(h_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{0}),h_{1}\big)_{L^{2}_{f}(M)}-p_{1},\dots,\big(h_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{0}),h_{K}\big)_{L^{2}_{f}(M)}-p_{K}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\geq|\mathbf{p}|-s\left|\left(\big(h_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{0}),h_{1}\big)_{L^{2}_{f}(M)}-p_{1},\dots,\big(h_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{0}),h_{K}\big)_{L^{2}_{f}(M)}-p_{K}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\geq 2\mu_{u}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}-C(n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*})e^{-\frac{\gamma_{0}}{100}e^{\tau_{0}}}

(
8.1
)

\displaystyle>0

\displaystyle(\tau_{0}\gg 1).

Therefore,
\mathcal{F}(\overline{A})=\mathcal{F}_{s=1}(\overline{A})\subset\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{0\}

and the straight-line homotopy
\mathcal{F}_{s}
gives a homotopy from the identity
Id:\overline{A}\to\overline{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
to
\mathcal{F}|_{\overline{A}}:\overline{A}\to\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
.
∎

Lemma 8.9
.

If
0<\overline{p}\ll 1
is sufficiently small (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*}
),

0<\epsilon_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*}
),

0<\mu_{u},\mu_{s}\ll 1
are sufficiently small (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*}
),

0<\gamma_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}
),
and

\tau_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\overline{p},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\gamma_{0},\mu_{u},\mu_{s}
),
then

t_{\mu_{u},1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})<t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})

for all
|\mathbf{p}|\leq\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}
.

Proof.

We first show
t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})<t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
.
Observe that the assumptions of Theorem
6.1
hold
with
\tau_{1}=\tau(t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*})=-\ln(1-t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*})
,

\epsilon=\epsilon_{0}
,
and
\mu=\sqrt{\mu_{u}^{2}+\mu_{s}^{2}}.

Let
W=W(n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*})
and
W^{\prime}=W^{\prime}(n,M,\overline{g},f)\geq 1
be as in Theorem
6.1
and set
\delta=\frac{\min\{\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\}}{2W^{\prime}}\in(0,\epsilon_{0}]
.
Then Theorem
6.1
implies

(8.9)

\displaystyle|h_{\mathbf{p}}|+|\overline{\nabla}h_{\mathbf{p}}|+|\overline{\nabla}^{2}h_{\mathbf{p}}|\leq Wf^{|\lambda_{*}|}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau}\text{ and }

(8.10)

\displaystyle|h_{\mathbf{p}}|+|\overline{\nabla}h_{\mathbf{p}}|+|\overline{\nabla}^{2}h_{\mathbf{p}}|\leq W^{\prime}\delta=\frac{1}{2}\min\{\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\}

throughout
M\times[\tau_{0},\tau(t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*})]

so long as

0<\overline{p}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*}
;

0<\epsilon=\epsilon_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*}
;

0<\mu_{u},\mu_{s}\ll 1
are sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*}
;

0<\gamma_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}
;
and
\tau_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\overline{p},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\gamma_{0}
.

In particular, (
8.10
) implies

h_{\mathbf{p}}\in\mathcal{B}\left[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u},\mu_{s},\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{0},\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{1},\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{2},[t_{0},t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}]\right].

By Lemma
8.5
, for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}^{+})
,

\supp h_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\subset\{f\leq\Gamma_{0}(1-t)^{-1}\}\subset\{f\leq\Gamma_{0}(1-t_{1}^{+})^{-1}\}\Subset M

since
t_{1}^{+}<1
.
Since
h
is smooth,
it follows that, for all
t_{2}
in a neighborhood of
t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}
,

(8.11)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} h_{\mathbf{p}}&\in\mathcal{B}\left[\lambda_{*},\frac{3}{2}\mu_{u},\frac{3}{2}\mu_{s},\frac{3}{4}\epsilon_{0},\frac{3}{4}\epsilon_{1},\frac{3}{4}\epsilon_{2},[t_{0},t_{2})\right]\\
&\subset\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},2,2,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},[t_{0},t_{2})]\end{aligned}

if
0<\mu_{u},\mu_{s}\leq\frac{4}{3}
.
Thus,
t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})\geq t_{2}>t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
.

Next, we prove that
t_{\mu_{u},1}^{*}=t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}
.
First, note that Lemma
5.4
, Lemma
8.5
, and equations (
8.9
) and (
8.10
)
imply the assumptions of Lemma
7.1
hold.
Thus,

\|\mathcal{E}_{1}\|_{L^{2}_{f}}+\|\mathcal{E}_{2}\|_{L^{2}_{f}}\lesssim_{n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*}}e^{2\lambda_{*}\tau}

for all
\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau(t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*})]

when
0<\epsilon_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n
and
\tau_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\gamma_{0}
.
Additionally, Lemma
8.1
implies that,
if
\tau_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*},\gamma_{0},\mu_{s}
, then

\displaystyle\|\pi_{s}h_{\mathbf{p}}(\tau_{0})\|_{L^{2}_{f}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{3}\mu_{s}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}.

Lemma
7.3
on
[\tau_{0},\tau(t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*})]

now implies

\displaystyle\|\pi_{s}h_{\mathbf{p}}(\tau)\|_{L^{2}_{f}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{3}\mu_{s}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau}

for all
\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau(t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*})]
.
By smoothness of
h
, it now follows that (
8.11
) can be improved to

(8.12)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} h_{\mathbf{p}}&\in\mathcal{B}\left[\lambda_{*},2,\frac{3}{4}\mu_{s},\frac{3}{4}\epsilon_{0},\frac{3}{4}\epsilon_{1},\frac{3}{4}\epsilon_{2},t_{0},t_{2}\right]\\
&\subset\mathcal{B}\left[\lambda_{*},2,\mu_{s},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t_{0},t_{2}\right]\end{aligned}

for all
t_{2}
in a neighborhood of
t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}
.
Therefore,
t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}=t_{\mu_{u},1}^{*}
.
∎

Lemma 8.10
.

Under the same assumptions as Lemma
8.9
,

\mathcal{F}:\overline{B_{\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}}}\to\mathbb{R}^{K}
is continuous and

\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{p})\neq\mathbf{0}\qquad\text{for all }|\mathbf{p}|\leq\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}.

Proof.

By the definition of
t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}=t_{\mu_{u},1}^{*}
and (
8.11
) in the proof of Lemma
8.9
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} h_{\mathbf{p}}&\in\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u},1,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},[t_{0},t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}]],\\
h_{\mathbf{p}}&\notin\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u},1,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},[t_{0},t_{2}]],\text{ and }\\
h_{\mathbf{p}}&\in\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},2,2,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},[t_{0},t_{2}]]\end{aligned}

for all
t_{2}
in a neighborhood of
t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}
with
t_{2}>t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}
.
By Definition
4.21
of
\mathcal{B}
and the smoothness of
h_{\mathbf{p}}
, it follows that

(8.13)

|\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{p})|=\|\pi_{u}h_{\mathbf{p}}(t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*})\|_{L^{2}_{f}}=\mu_{u}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau(t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*})}>0.

For the continuity of
\mathcal{F}
, observe that (
8.13
) and Lemma
7.3
implies
that for arbitrary
\tau^{\prime},\tau^{\prime\prime}
in a neighborhood of
\tau(t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*})

with
\tau^{\prime}<\tau(t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*})<\tau^{\prime\prime}

\|\pi_{u}h_{\mathbf{p}}(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}_{f}}<\mu_{u}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau^{\prime}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\|\pi_{u}h_{\mathbf{p}}(\tau^{\prime\prime})\|_{L^{2}_{f}}>\mu_{u}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau^{\prime\prime}}.

(
8.12
) therefore implies that
h_{\mathbf{p}}(\tau^{\prime})
is in the interior of

\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u},\mu_{s},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\{\tau^{\prime}\}]

and
h_{\mathbf{p}}(\tau^{\prime\prime})
is in the interior of the complement of

\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u},\mu_{s},\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\{\tau^{\prime\prime}\}]
.
The same holds for all
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}
in a neighborhood of
\mathbf{p}
by continuity and thus

t(\tau^{\prime})\leq t_{\mu_{u},\mu_{s}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime})\leq t(\tau^{\prime\prime})
.
Continuity of
\mathcal{F}
then follows from the definition of
\mathcal{F}
.
∎

We may now prove main theorem of the paper.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

By Lemmas
8.8
–
8.10
with parameters chosen sufficiently large or small as in the assumptions of these lemmas,

\mathcal{F}:\overline{B_{\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}}}\to\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{0\}

is a continuous function whose restriction to
\overline{A}=\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}:2\mu_{u}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}\leq|\mathbf{p}|\leq\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}\}

is homotopic to the identity
Id:\overline{A}\to\overline{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{0\}
.
However, such a map cannot exist as
Id:\overline{A}\to\overline{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{K}\setminus\{0\}
is not null-homotopic.
Therefore, the assumption (in assumption
8.6
) that
t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})<1
for all
|\mathbf{p}|\leq\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}
must be false.
Hence, there exists
\mathbf{p}^{*}
with
|\mathbf{p}^{*}|\leq\overline{p}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau_{0}}
such that

1=t_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{*})=\sup\{t_{1}\in(t_{0},1]:\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}[\lambda_{*},\overline{p},\Gamma_{0},\gamma_{0},\mu_{u}=2,\mu_{s}=2,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t_{0},t_{1}]\}.

Therefore, there exists a sequence
t^{(k)}\nearrow 1
and smooth families of diffeomorphisms
\tilde{\Phi}^{(k)}:M\times[t_{0},t^{(k)})\to M
solving the harmonic map heat flow (
4.7
) such that

h_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t)=\frac{1}{1-t}((\Phi_{t}^{(k)})^{-1})^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t)-\overline{g}\in\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u}=2,\mu_{s}=2,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t_{0},t^{(k)}].

With
0<\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1}\ll 1
sufficiently small depending on
n
,
Proposition
B.6
implies that, for any
k,k^{\prime}
,
\tilde{\Phi}^{(k)}=\tilde{\Phi}^{(k^{\prime})}
on the intersection of their domains.
Hence, the
\tilde{\Phi}^{(k)}
paste to define a smooth solution
\tilde{\Phi}:M\times[t_{0},1)\to M
of the harmonic map heat flow (
4.7
) which is a diffeomorphism on each time slice and satisfies

h_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t)=\frac{1}{1-t}(\Phi_{t}^{-1})^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t)-\overline{g}\in\mathcal{B}[\lambda_{*},\mu_{u}=2,\mu_{s}=2,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},t_{0},1].

By Definition
4.21
of
\mathcal{B}
,
h_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t)
remains in a small
C^{2}(M)
-neighborhood of
0
for all
t
and converges in
L^{2}_{f}(M)
to
0
as
t\nearrow 1
.
By similar logic as in the beginning of the proof of Lemma
8.9
, Theorem
6.1
applies to show

|h_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(\tau)|+|\overline{\nabla}h_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(\tau)|+|\overline{\nabla}^{2}h_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(\tau)|\lesssim_{n,M,\overline{g},f,\lambda_{*}}f^{|\lambda_{*}|}e^{\lambda_{*}\tau}\qquad\text{for all $\tau\in[\tau_{0},\infty)$.}

Therefore,
h_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t)
converges in
C^{2}_{loc}(M)
to
0
as
t\nearrow 1
.
The local interior estimates in Lemma
6.2
then bootstrap this to convergence in
C^{\infty}_{loc}(M)
.

Recall from Remark
4.11
and the choice of
\eta_{\Gamma_{0}}
that

\iota^{*}\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t)=G_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t)\quad\text{ on }\left\{f<\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}\right\}\subset\iota^{-1}\left(\bigsqcup_{\omega\in A}\{x\in M:\eta_{\Gamma_{0}}(x)=1\}\right)\subset\mathcal{M}.

We claim that, for any
t\in[t_{0},1)
,

(8.14)

(\varphi_{t}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{t}\circ\iota)\left(\left\{f<\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}\right\}\right)\supset\{f<(1-t)^{-1}\}.

Indeed, let
t\in[t_{0},1)
and let
x\in M
with
f(x)\geq\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}
. By similar logic as in the beginning of the proof of Lemma
5.5
,
the local drift estimate of Lemma
B.5
can be applied to deduce

f(\tilde{\Phi}_{t}(x))\geq\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{0}

so long as
\Gamma_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f
;

0<\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1}\ll 1
are sufficiently small depending on
n
;
and
0<1-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,\Gamma_{0}
.
Therefore, for all
t\in[t_{0},1)
,

\tilde{\Phi}_{t}\left(\left\{x\in M:f(x)\geq\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}\right\}\right)\subset\left\{x\in M:f(x)\geq\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{0}\right\}.

The proof of Lemma
5.5
shows

\varphi_{t}(\{f\geq\Gamma_{0}/2\})\subset\{f\geq(1-t)^{-1}\}\qquad\text{ for all }t\in[0,1)

when
\Gamma_{0}\gg 1
is sufficiently large depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f
.
Hence,

(\varphi_{t}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{t})\left(\left\{f\geq\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}\right\}\right)\subset\varphi_{t}\left(\left\{f\geq\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{0}\right\}\right)\subset\{f\geq(1-t)^{-1}\}\quad\text{ for all }t\in[t_{0},1).

Taking complements and using the fact that
\varphi_{t},\tilde{\Phi}_{t}
are bijections for all
t\in[t_{0},1)

then implies

(\varphi_{t}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{t}\circ\iota)\left(\left\{f<\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}\right\}\right)=(\varphi_{t}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{t})\left(\left\{f<\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}\right\}\right)\supset\{f<(1-t)^{-1}\}

for all
t\in[t_{0},1)
.
This proves the claim (
8.14
).

(
8.14
), the uniform
C^{2}(M)
bounds on
h_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t)
, and the fact that
h_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t)
converges to
0
in
C^{\infty}_{loc}(M)
as
t\nearrow 1

together show that
the curvature of
G_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t)
blows up at the type I rate on
\left\{f<\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}\right\}\subset\mathcal{M}
, that is,

0<\limsup_{t\nearrow 1}\sup_{x\in\{f<\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}\}\subset\mathcal{M}}(1-t)|Rm_{G_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}}|_{G_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}}(x,t)<\infty,

and also complete the proof of the second statement of Theorem
1.1

with
U=\left\{f<\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}\right\}
.
Note that Proposition
5.2
additionally applies to give curvature bounds on
\mathcal{M}\setminus\left\{f<\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}\right\}
and confirm the singularity is local.

To conclude, we check that Cheeger-Gromov convergence follows.
Let
t_{j}
be a sequence of times with
t_{j}\nearrow 1
and let
y_{\infty}\in M
.
By (
8.14
),
the sets
(\varphi_{t_{j}}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{t_{j}}\circ\iota)\left(\left\{f<\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}\right\}\right)\subset M

give an exhaustion of
M

and there exists a sequence
x_{j}\in\mathcal{M}
such that
x_{j}\in\{f<\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}\}
and

(\varphi_{t_{j}}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{t_{j}}\circ\iota)(x_{j})=y_{\infty}
for all
j
large enough.
Consider the sequence of parabolically rescaled Ricci flows

G_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}^{(j)}(t)=\frac{1}{1-t_{j}}G_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t_{j}+t(1-t_{j}))\qquad\text{ on }\mathcal{M}\times\left[1-\frac{1-t_{0}}{1-t_{j}},1\right).

The fact that
h_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t)
converges to
0
in
C^{\infty}_{loc}(M)
as
t\nearrow 1
implies

(\mathcal{M},G_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}^{(j)}(0),x_{j})\xrightarrow[j\nearrow\infty]{}(M,\overline{g},y_{\infty})

in the pointed Cheeger-Gromov sense
with the maps
(\varphi_{t_{j}}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{t_{j}}\circ\iota)^{-1}
defined on
(\varphi_{t_{j}}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{t_{j}}\circ\iota)\left(\left\{f<\frac{4}{6}\Gamma_{0}\right\}\right)\subset M
.
The type I curvature blow up rate of
(\mathcal{M},G_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t))
implies that, for any
-\infty<a<b<1
, the sequence of parabolically rescaled Ricci flows
\left(\mathcal{M},G^{(j)}_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t)\right)
has curvature bounds on
\mathcal{M}\times[a,b]
that are uniform in
j
.
By
[
CCG+07
, Chapter 3, Section 2]
, this then gives sufficient compactness to deduce the
flows

\left(\mathcal{M},G^{(j)}_{\mathbf{p}^{*}}(t),x_{j}\right)
subsequentially converge to the flow
(M,(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g},y_{\infty})
in the pointed Cheeger-Gromov sense.
∎

Appendix A
Rounding Out Cones

Recall from Definition
2.24
that

\mathcal{C}_{R}(\Sigma)=(R,\infty)\times\Sigma\quad\text{and}\quad g_{\mathcal{C}}=dr^{2}+r^{2}g_{\Sigma}

where
(\Sigma,g_{\Sigma})
is a closed Riemannian manifold.
The goal of this appendix is to prove Proposition
A.7
which explicitly constructs manifolds
\mathcal{M}
as in Subsection
4.1
and metrics
G_{\mathbf{0}}(t_{0})
on
\mathcal{M}
satisfying Definition
4.2
for any given (smooth, complete) asymptotically conical shrinker
(M,\overline{g},f)
.
Informally,
\mathcal{M}
is the double of a suitably large subset of
M
and has a
\mathcal{S}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry given by interchanging the two pieces of the double.
The metrics
G_{\mathbf{0}}(t_{0})
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-invariant metrics that, on each piece of the double, spatially interpolate from the soliton metric
(1-t_{0})\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}
to the cone metric
g_{\mathcal{C}}
to a cylindrical metric of large radius.
The spatial interpolations can be done without increasing the curvature too much or collapsing the volume of balls along the interpolation.

Throughout this appendix,
(M,\overline{g},f)
continues to denote a smooth, complete, asymptotically conical, gradient shrinking Ricci soliton.
The first proposition here comes from
[
KW15
, Proposition 2.1]
and its proof adapted to the case of multiple ends and with the diffeomorphisms
\Psi_{t}
specified further.

Proposition A.1

(Proposition 2.1 of
[
KW15
]
)
.

Let
(M,\overline{g},f)
be a shrinker which is asymptotic to the (regular) cone

\left(\mathcal{C}(\Sigma),g_{\mathcal{C}}\right)
along an end
V
(see Definition
2.26
).

Then there exists
R_{0}>0

and a smooth family of maps
\Psi_{t}=\varphi_{t}\circ\Psi:\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}(\Sigma)\to V

such that

(1)

For all
t\in[0,1)
,
\Psi_{t}=\varphi_{t}\circ\Psi
is a diffeomorphism onto its image and

\Psi_{t}(\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}(\Sigma))
is an end of
M
,

(2)

(1-t)\Psi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}=(1-t)\Psi^{*}\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}
extends smoothly as
t\nearrow 1
to
g_{\mathcal{C}}
on
\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}(\Sigma)
, and

(3)

the function
\Psi^{*}f:\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies

r^{2}-\frac{N_{0}}{r^{2}}\leq 4\Psi^{*}f\leq r^{2}+\frac{N_{0}}{r^{2}}.

In particular,

(1-t)\Psi^{*}\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}\xrightarrow[t\nearrow 1]{C^{\infty}_{loc}(\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}(\Sigma),g_{\mathcal{C}})}g_{\mathcal{C}}.

Remark A.2
.

By taking
R_{0}
slightly larger in Proposition
A.1
, we may assume without loss of generality that the conclusions apply on the set
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{R}(\Sigma)}=[R_{0},\infty)\times\Sigma
.
Proposition
A.1
also extends naturally to the case of finitely many asymptotically conical ends.

Let
(\Sigma^{n-1},g_{\Sigma})
be a closed Riemannian manifold.
Consider the warped product

I\times_{\psi}\Sigma\quad\text{ with metric }dr^{2}+\psi(r)^{2}g_{\Sigma}

where
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is an interval with coordinate
r
.

Observe that
\psi\equiv r
gives a cone metric and a constant warping function
\psi\equiv C
gives a cylinder metric.
The next result says how to interpolate between these two metrics without increasing the curvature too much.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
(\Sigma^{n-1},g_{\Sigma})
be a closed Riemannian manifold
and let
R>0
.
There exists a smooth function
\psi_{R}:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
,
\psi_{R}(r)
,
such that

\psi_{R}
is non-decreasing,

\psi_{R}(r)\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}=r,&\text{ if }0<r\leq R,\\
\in[R,2R],&\text{ if }R<r<3R,\\
=2R,&\text{ if }3R\leq r,\\
\end{array}\right.

and the curvature of
(0,\infty)\times_{\psi_{R}}\Sigma
with the associated warped product metric
g=g_{R}=dr^{2}+\psi_{R}(r)^{2}g_{\Sigma}

satisfies the estimate

|{}^{g}\nabla^{m}Rm[g]|_{g}\lesssim_{n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma},m}\frac{1}{R^{2+m}}\qquad\text{on }\mathcal{C}_{R/2}(\Sigma).

Additionally, there exists
c=c(n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma})>0
such that

Vol_{g_{R}}(B_{g_{R}}(x,1))\geq c>0\qquad\forall x\in\mathcal{C}_{R/2}(\Sigma),\,\forall R\geq 1.

Proof.

There exists a smooth, non-decreasing function
\psi_{1}:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)

such that

\psi_{1}(r)\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}=r,&\text{ if }0<r\leq 1,\\
\in[1,2],&\text{ if }1<r<3,\text{ and}\\
=2,&\text{ if }3\leq r.\\
\end{array}\right.

Since
\psi_{1}
is smooth and
\Sigma
is closed, for each
m
there exists a constant
C_{m}
so that

\left|\frac{d^{m}\psi_{1}}{dr^{m}}\right|\leq C_{m}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\left|{}^{\Sigma}\nabla^{m}Rm_{\Sigma}\right|_{\Sigma}\leq C_{m}.

It follows that the warped product metric

g_{1}\doteqdot dr^{2}+\psi_{1}(r)^{2}g_{\Sigma}

satisfies curvature estimates of the form

|{}^{g_{1}}\nabla^{m}Rm[g_{1}]|_{g_{1}}\leq C(n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma},m)\qquad\text{on }\mathcal{C}_{1/2}(\Sigma).

By scaling, the warped product metric

g_{R}\doteqdot R^{2}g_{1}=R^{2}dr^{2}+[R\psi_{1}(r)]^{2}g_{\Sigma}=d\tilde{r}^{2}+\left[R\psi_{1}\left(\tilde{r}/R\right)\right]^{2}g_{\Sigma}\qquad(\tilde{r}\doteqdot Rr)

has curvature bounds and warping function
\psi_{R}(\tilde{r})\doteqdot R\psi_{1}(\tilde{r}/R)
as claimed in the statement of the lemma.

It remains to show
g_{R}
satisfies the volume lower bound claimed in the statement of the lemma.
First, observe that there exists
c=c(n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma})>0
such that

Vol_{g_{1}}B_{g_{1}}(x,1/4)\geq c>0\qquad\forall x\in\mathcal{C}_{1/2}(\Sigma),

and
B_{g_{1}}(x,1/4)\subset\mathcal{C}_{1/4}(\Sigma)
for all
x\in\mathcal{C}_{1/2}(\Sigma)
.
By curvature bounds for
g_{1}
on
\mathcal{C}_{1/4}(\Sigma)
and volume monotonicity, it follows that

Vol_{g_{1}}B_{g_{1}}(x,r)\geq c^{\prime}r^{n}\qquad\forall x\in\mathcal{C}_{1/2}(\Sigma),\,\forall 0<r\leq\frac{1}{4}

for some constant
c^{\prime}=c^{\prime}(n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma})>0
.
Therefore, by scaling
g_{R}=R^{2}g_{1}
,

Vol_{g_{R}}B_{g_{R}}(x,1)\geq Vol_{g_{R}}B_{g_{R}}(x,1/4)=R^{n}Vol_{g_{1}}B_{g_{1}}\left(x,\frac{1}{4R}\right)\\
\geq R^{n}c^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{4R}\right)^{n}=\frac{c^{\prime}}{4^{n}}>0

for all
x\in\mathcal{C}_{R/2}(\Sigma)
and all
R\geq 1
.
This completes the proof.
∎

The next lemma looks at an interpolation between the metrics from Proposition
A.1
and Lemma
A.3
. It provides estimates for this new metric.

Lemma A.4
.

Let
(M^{n},\overline{g},f)
be a shrinker which is asymptotic to the cone
(\mathcal{C}(\Sigma),g_{\mathcal{C}})
.
Consider
R_{0}>0
and
\Psi_{t}=\Psi\circ\varphi_{t}
as in Proposition
A.1
.
Let
R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
be such that

0<R_{0}<R_{1}<R_{2}<R_{2}+2<R_{3}.

Let
\eta(r):(0,\infty)\to[0,1]
be a smooth bump function that decreases from 1 to 0 over the interval
(R_{1},R_{2})
.
For all
0\leq t_{0}<1
, consider the metric
\check{G}(t_{0})
on
\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}(\Sigma)
given by

\check{G}(t_{0})=\eta(1-t_{0})\Psi^{*}\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}+(1-\eta)g_{R_{3}}

where
g_{R_{3}}
is the warped product metric from Lemma
A.3
.

Then

(1)

\check{G}(t_{0})\xrightarrow[t_{0}\nearrow 1]{}g_{\mathcal{C}}\quad\text{in }C^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}(\Sigma)\setminus\overline{\mathcal{C}_{R_{3}}(\Sigma)},g_{\mathcal{C}}\right),

(2)

for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
if
0<1-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small, then

\check{G}(t_{0})
satisfies the curvature estimate

|\check{\nabla}^{m}\check{Rm}|_{\check{G}(t_{0})}\lesssim_{n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma},m}\max\left\{\frac{1}{r^{2+m}},\frac{1}{R_{3}^{2+m}}\right\}\qquad\text{on }\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}(\Sigma),

and

(3)

there exists
c=c(n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma})>0
such that

Vol_{\check{G}(t_{0})}B_{\check{G}(t_{0})}(x,1)\geq c>0\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}+1}(\Sigma)

for all
0<1-t_{0}\ll 1
sufficiently small.

Proof.

To simplify notation, for any
R<R^{\prime}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{R}^{R^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
denote the annular region

\mathcal{C}_{R}^{R^{\prime}}(\Sigma)\doteqdot\mathcal{C}_{R}(\Sigma)\setminus\overline{\mathcal{C}_{R^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}=(R,R^{\prime})\times\Sigma

and
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{R}^{R^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}
its closure in
\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)
,

\overline{\mathcal{C}_{R}^{R^{\prime}}(\Sigma)}=[R,R^{\prime}]\times\Sigma.

First, note that
\check{G}(t_{0})=g_{\mathcal{C}}
on
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{R_{2}}^{R_{3}}(\Sigma)}
by construction.
Thus, it suffices to prove convergence to
g_{\mathcal{C}}
on the region
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}^{R_{2}}(\Sigma)}
.
Here, we can write

\check{G}(t_{0})=\eta(1-t_{0})\Psi^{*}\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}+(1-\eta)g_{R_{3}}=\eta(g_{\mathcal{C}}+h(t_{0}))+(1-\eta)g_{\mathcal{C}}=g_{\mathcal{C}}+\eta h(t_{0})

where

h(t_{0})=(1-t_{0})\Psi^{*}\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}-g_{\mathcal{C}}.

By Proposition
A.1
,

(1-t_{0})\Psi^{*}\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}
converges to
g_{\mathcal{C}}
in
C^{\infty}_{loc}(\overline{\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}(\Sigma)},g_{\mathcal{C}})
as
t_{0}\nearrow 1
.
Hence,
\eta h(t_{0})
converges to
0
in
C^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}^{R_{2}}(\Sigma)},g_{\mathcal{C}})
as
t_{0}\nearrow 1
.
This proves the first conclusion (
1
).

Next, we show the curvature estimates (
2
).
Observe that, by construction,
\check{G}(t_{0})\equiv g_{R_{3}}
on the set
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{R_{2}}(\Sigma)}\subset\{(r,\sigma)\in\mathcal{C}(\Sigma):\eta(r)=0\}
.
Therefore, the curvature estimates from Lemma
A.3
imply that

\left|\check{\nabla}^{m}\check{Rm}\right|\lesssim_{n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma},m}\frac{1}{R_{3}^{2+m}}\qquad\text{on }\overline{\mathcal{C}_{R_{3}}(\Sigma)}.

It remains to obtain the curvature estimate on
\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}^{R_{3}}(\Sigma)
.
Note that the corresponding curvature estimate holds for
g_{\mathcal{C}}
, that is,

\left|{}^{g_{\mathcal{C}}}\nabla^{m}Rm_{\mathcal{C}}\right|_{g_{\mathcal{C}}}(r,\sigma)\lesssim_{n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma},m}\frac{1}{r^{2+m}}\qquad\text{for all }(r,\sigma)\in\overline{\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}(\Sigma)}.

\check{G}(t_{0})

g_{\mathcal{C}}
-smoothly converges to
g_{\mathcal{C}}
on
\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}^{R_{3}}(\Sigma)
.
It therefore follows that, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, if
0<1-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small, then

|\check{\nabla}^{m}\check{Rm}|_{\check{G}(t_{0})}(r,\sigma)\lesssim_{n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma},m}\frac{1}{r^{2+m}}\qquad\text{for all }(r,\sigma)\in\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}^{R_{3}}(\Sigma).

This completes the proof of the curvature estimates (
2
).

Finally, we show the volume lower bound (
3
).
Since the cone
(\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}(\Sigma),g_{\mathcal{C}})
satisfies a similar volume lower bound and
\check{G}(t_{0})
converges to
g_{\mathcal{C}}
in
\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}^{R_{3}}(\Sigma)
, it follows that there exists
c=c(n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma})>0
such that

(A.1)

Vol_{\check{G}(t_{0})}B_{\check{G}(t_{0})}(x,1)\geq c>0\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}+1}(\Sigma)

for all
0<1-t_{0}\ll 1
.
Since
R_{3}>R_{2}+2\geq 1
, Lemma
A.3
implies there exists
c^{\prime}=c^{\prime}(n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma})>0
such that

(A.2)

Vol_{\check{G}(t_{0})}B_{\check{G}(t_{0})}(x,1)=Vol_{g_{R_{3}}}B_{g_{R_{3}}}(x,1)\geq c^{\prime}>0\\
\text{for all }x\in\mathcal{C}_{\max\{R_{3}/2,R_{2}+1\}}(\Sigma).

2<R_{2}+2<R_{3}
implies
\max\{R_{3}/2,R_{2}+1\}<R_{3}-1
, and thus (
A.1
) and (
A.2
) imply

Vol_{\check{G}(t_{0})}B_{\check{G}(t_{0})}(x,1)\geq\min\{c,c^{\prime}\}>0\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}+1}(\Sigma).

This completes the proof of item (
3
) in the statement of the lemma.
∎

Let
\check{G}(t_{0}),\Psi,R_{0}
be as in Lemma
A.4
and
consider now
(\Psi^{-1})^{*}\check{G}(t_{0})
on
\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}(\Sigma))\subset M
.
This metric can be extended by
(1-t_{0})\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}
on the complement of
\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}(\Sigma))
to yield a Riemannian metric on
M
.
We denote this metric by
G(t_{0})
.

We first record the following lemma comparing regions in
M
and
\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}
.

Lemma A.5
.

If
R\gg 1
is sufficiently large (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,N_{0},R_{0}
, and
\Psi
from Proposition
A.1
), then

\left\{x\in M:f(x)>\frac{R^{2}}{2}\right\}\subset\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R})\subset\left\{x\in M:f(x)>\frac{R^{2}}{8}\right\}.

Proof.

We first claim that there exists
\Gamma_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\{x\in M:f(x)>\Gamma_{*}\}\subset\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}).

By Proposition
A.1
,
\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}})
is a union of the ends of
M
.
Hence, there exists
\Omega\Subset M
such that
M\setminus\Omega\subset\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}})
.
Since
\Omega\Subset M
, there exists
\Gamma_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\Omega\subset\{x\in M:f(x)\leq\Gamma_{*}\}.

It follows that

\{x\in M:f(x)>\Gamma_{*}\}\subset M\setminus\Omega\subset\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}).

Now, let
x\in\mathcal{C}_{R}
.
By Proposition
A.1
,

\displaystyle f(\Psi(x))\geq\frac{1}{4}\left(r(x)^{2}-\frac{N_{0}}{r(x)^{2}}\right)>\frac{1}{4}\left(R^{2}-\frac{N_{0}}{R^{2}}\right)\geq\frac{R^{2}}{8}

if
R\geq(2N_{0}^{2})^{1/4}
.
Hence, if
R\geq(2N_{0}^{2})^{1/4}
, then

\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R})\subset\left\{x\in M:f(x)>\frac{R^{2}}{8}\right\}.

For the other inclusion, assume
x\in M
and
f(x)>\frac{R^{2}}{2}
.
If
\frac{R^{2}}{2}>\Gamma_{*}
, then
x=\Psi(x^{\prime})
for some
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}
.
It follows from Proposition
A.1
that

\displaystyle r(x^{\prime})^{2}+\frac{N_{0}}{R_{0}^{2}}>r(x^{\prime})^{2}+\frac{N_{0}}{r(x^{\prime})^{2}}\geq 4f(\Psi(x^{\prime}))=4f(x)>2R^{2}.

If also
R^{2}\geq\frac{N_{0}}{R_{0}^{2}}
, then

r(x^{\prime})>\sqrt{2R^{2}-\frac{N_{0}}{R_{0}^{2}}}\geq R.

It follows that for such
R
,

\{x\in M:f(x)>\frac{R^{2}}{2}\}\subset\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R}).

∎

Proposition A.6
.

Consider the metric
G(t_{0})
on
M
which consists of
(\Psi^{-1})^{*}\check{G}(t_{0})
extended by
(1-t_{0})\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}
.
If
\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2}}R_{3}=\frac{1}{2}R_{2}=R_{1}\gg 1
is sufficiently large (depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,N_{0},R_{0},\Psi
),
then the following hold:

(1)

for all
0\leq t_{0},t_{0}^{\prime}<1
,

G(t_{0})=G(t_{0}^{\prime})\qquad\text{on }\left\{x\in M:f(x)>2R_{1}^{2}\right\},

(2)

G(t_{0})=(1-t_{0})\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}\qquad\text{on }\left\{x\in M:f(x)\leq\frac{R_{1}^{2}}{8}\right\},

(3)

G(t_{0})
smoothly converges to
(\Psi^{-1})^{*}g_{\mathcal{C}}
in the region

\left\{x\in M:\frac{R_{1}^{2}}{16}<f(x)<4R_{1}^{2}\right\}\subset\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}})

as
t_{0}\nearrow 1
,

(4)

for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, if
0<1-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small then

|\nabla^{m}Rm|_{G(t_{0})}\lesssim_{n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma},m}\frac{1}{R_{1}^{m+2}}\qquad\text{on }\{x\in M:f(x)>R_{1}^{2}/16\},

and

(5)

there exists
c=c(n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma})>0
such that

Vol_{G(t_{0})}B_{G(t_{0})}(x,1)\geq c>0\qquad\text{for all }x\in\{x\in M:f(x)>R_{1}^{2}/16\}

for all
0<1-t_{0}\ll 1
sufficiently small.

Proof.

Item (
1
) follows from the fact that, for all
0\leq t_{0},t_{0}^{\prime}<1
,

\check{G}(t_{0})=\check{G}(t_{0}^{\prime})\qquad\text{ on }\mathcal{C}_{R_{2}}

by construction and the fact that

\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R_{2}})\supset\left\{x\in M:f(x)>\frac{R_{2}^{2}}{2}\right\}=\{x\in M:f(x)>2R_{1}^{2}\}

by Lemma
A.5
if
R_{1}\gg 1
is sufficiently large.

For item (
2
), observe that

\check{G}(t_{0})=(1-t_{0})\Psi^{*}\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}\qquad\text{on }\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}^{R_{1}}

by construction.
It then follows from the definition of
G(t_{0})
and Lemma
A.5
that, if
R_{1}\gg 1
is sufficiently large, then

G(t_{0})\equiv(1-t_{0})\varphi_{t_{0}}^{*}\overline{g}\qquad\text{on }\left\{x\in M:f(x)\leq\frac{R_{1}^{2}}{8}\right\}.

Next, we prove item (
3
).
By Lemma
A.4
,

\check{G}(t_{0})
smoothly converges to
g_{\mathcal{C}}
on
\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}^{R_{3}}=\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}^{4\sqrt{2}R_{1}}
.
If
R_{1}\gg 1
is sufficiently large, then
\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}^{4\sqrt{2}R_{1}}\supset\mathcal{C}_{R_{1}/\sqrt{8}}^{4\sqrt{2}R_{1}}
.
Lemma
A.5
then implies that

G(t_{0})
smoothly converges to
(\Psi^{-1})^{*}g_{\mathcal{C}}
on

\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R_{1}/\sqrt{8}}^{4\sqrt{2}R_{1}})\supset\left\{x\in M:\frac{R_{1}^{2}}{16}<f(x)<4R_{1}^{2}\right\}

as
t_{0}\nearrow 1
.

Next, we prove item (
4
).
By Lemma
A.4
,
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, if
0<1-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small then

\check{G}(t_{0})
satisfies the curvature estimate

|\check{\nabla}^{m}\check{Rm}|_{\check{G}(t_{0})}\lesssim_{n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma},m}\frac{1}{R_{1}^{2+m}}\qquad\text{on }\mathcal{C}_{R_{1}/\sqrt{8}}=\mathcal{C}_{R_{3}/16}.

The corresponding estimate

|\nabla^{m}Rm|_{G(t_{0})}\lesssim_{n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma},m}\frac{1}{R_{1}^{2+m}}

therefore holds on

\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R_{1}/\sqrt{8}})\supset\left\{x\in M:f(x)>\frac{R_{1}^{2}}{16}\right\}

by Lemma
A.5
if
R_{1}\gg 1
is sufficiently large.

Finally, we prove item (
5
).
If
R_{1}\geq\sqrt{8}(R_{0}+1)
, then
\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}+1}(\Sigma)\supset\mathcal{C}_{R_{1}/\sqrt{8}}(\Sigma)
and
Lemma
A.4
implies there exists
c=c(n,\Sigma,g_{\Sigma})>0
such that

Vol_{\check{G}(t_{0})}B_{\check{G}(t_{0})}(x,1)\geq c>0\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathcal{C}_{R_{1}/\sqrt{8}}(\Sigma)

for all
0<1-t_{0}\ll 1
.
Thus, by Lemma
A.5
, the corresponding estimate
holds on

\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{R_{1}/\sqrt{8}})\supset\left\{x\in M:f(x)>\frac{R_{1}^{2}}{16}\right\}

if
R_{1}\gg 1
is sufficiently large.
This proves item (
5
) and completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Proposition A.7
.

Let
(M,\overline{g},f)
be a smooth, complete, asymptotically conical, gradient, shrinking Ricci soliton which is asymptotic to the cone
(\mathcal{C}(\Sigma),g_{\mathcal{C}})
.
Then there exists a closed manifold
\mathcal{M}
satisfying the conditions of Subsection
4.1
with
\mathcal{S}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
A=\{0,1\}
, and there exists a family of metrics
G_{\mathbf{0}}(t_{0})=G_{\mathbf{0}}(\Gamma_{0},t_{0})
on
\mathcal{M}
satisfying Definition
4.2
.

Proof.

Fix
R_{0}>0
and

\Psi:\mathcal{C}_{R_{0}}(\Sigma)\xrightarrow{\cong}V\subset M

a diffeomorphism as in Proposition
A.1
.
For any
R>R_{0}
, define

M_{R}\doteqdot(M\setminus V)\cup\left(\Psi((R_{0},R)\times\Sigma)\right)\subset M.

Note that
M_{R}\subset M
is an open submanifold with closure

\overline{M}_{R}=(M\setminus V)\cup\left(\Psi((R_{0},R]\times\Sigma)\right)\subset M

which is a compact manifold with boundary
\partial\overline{M}_{R}\cong\Sigma
.
Let
\mathcal{M}_{R}
be the double of
\overline{M}_{R}
, that is,

\mathcal{M}_{R}\doteqdot(\overline{M}_{R}\times\{0\})\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x,0)\sim(x,1)\\
\text{ for }x\in\partial\overline{M}_{R}\end{subarray}}(\overline{M}_{R}\times\{1\}).

Then
\mathcal{M}_{R}
inherits a topology and smooth structure from
\overline{M}_{R}
which makes
\mathcal{M}_{R}
into a closed manifold.
Additionally, the map
\mathcal{M}_{R}\to\mathcal{M}_{R}
given by
(x,i\mod 2)\mapsto(x,i+1\mod 2)
is a diffeomorphism of
\mathcal{M}_{R}
that generates a subgroup
\mathcal{S}_{R}\leq\text{Diff}(\mathcal{M}_{R})
such that
\mathcal{S}_{R}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
as groups.

There exists a diffeomorphism from
(R,R+1)
to
(R,\infty)
which is the identity near
R
, and this induces a diffeomorphism
\iota_{R+1}^{\prime\prime}:M_{R+1}\to M
for any
R>R_{0}
.
Note that
\iota_{R+1}^{\prime\prime}
is not the inclusion map
M_{R+1}\subset M
, but
\iota_{R+1}^{\prime\prime}
can be chosen so that it restricts to the inclusion map on
M_{R}
.
Thus, for any
R>R_{0}
,
M_{R+2}
contains a diffeomorphic copy of
M
given by
M_{R+1}\subset M_{R+2}
with diffeomorphism
\iota^{\prime\prime}_{R+1}:M_{R+1}\to M
.
Hence,
\mathcal{M}_{R+2}
contains two disjoint diffeomorphic copies
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{R+2}=\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{R+2,0}\sqcup\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{R+2,1}\subset\mathcal{M}_{R+2}
of
M
corresponding to the
\iota^{\prime\prime}_{R+1}:M_{R+1}\xrightarrow{\cong}M
in each piece of the double.
Clearly, the diffeomorphisms in
\mathcal{S}_{R+2}\leq\text{Diff}(\mathcal{M}_{R+2})
preserve these identifications with
M
.

Note that all this topological data
M_{R},\mathcal{M}_{R},\mathcal{S}_{R},\iota^{\prime\prime}_{R}
depends on
R>R_{0}
.
However, because
(R_{0},R)
is diffeomorphic to
(R_{0},R^{\prime})
for any
R,R^{\prime}\in(R_{0},\infty)
,
it follows that
M_{R}\cong M_{R^{\prime}}
,
\overline{M}_{R}\cong\overline{M}_{R^{\prime}}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{R}\cong\mathcal{M}_{R^{\prime}}
for all
R,R^{\prime}\in(R_{0},\infty)
.
Thus, there exists a
fixed
closed manifold
\mathcal{M}
and diffeomorphisms
F_{R}:\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{M}_{R}
for all
R>R_{0}
.
Moreover, the diffeomorphisms
F_{R+2}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{R+2}
can be chosen such that

\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}\doteqdot\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{0}\sqcup\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{1}\doteqdot F_{R+2}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{R+2,0})\sqcup F_{R+2}^{-2}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{R+2,1})\subset\mathcal{M}\text{ is indepedent of $R>R_{0}$,}

\text{and }\iota_{R+1}^{\prime\prime}\circ F_{R+2}|_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{R+2}}=\iota_{R^{\prime}+1}^{\prime\prime}\circ F_{R^{\prime}+2}|_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{R^{\prime}+2}}\quad\forall R,R^{\prime}>R_{0}.

For example, take
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{R_{0}+3}
, use the relation

\iota_{R_{0}+2}^{\prime\prime}\circ F_{R_{0}+3}|_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{R_{0}+3}}=\iota_{R+1}^{\prime\prime}\circ F_{R+2}|_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{R+2}}

to define
F_{R+2}
on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{R+2}\subset\mathcal{M}_{R+2}
, and extend this to a diffeomorphism of
\mathcal{M}_{R+2}
to
\mathcal{M}_{R_{0}+3}
using a suitable diffeomorphism between the intervals
(R+1,R+2)
and
(R_{0}+2,R_{0}+3)
.

Therefore, by pulling back the
\iota^{\prime\prime}_{R+1}
to the manifold
\mathcal{M}
via the diffeomorphisms
F_{R+2}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{R+2}
, we have a closed manifold
\mathcal{M}
with two disjoint diffeomorphic copies
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{0}\sqcup\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{1}
of
M
and
fixed
diffeomorphisms
\iota_{i}^{\prime\prime}:\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\prime\prime}\to M

(i=0,1)
which are independent of
R>R_{0}
.
Since the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
\mathcal{M}_{R+2}
interchanges the two pieces of the double, this action also pulls back by
F_{R+2}
to give a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on
\mathcal{M}
by diffeomorphisms which is independent of
R>R_{0}
, interchanges the
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, and respects the identifications
\iota_{i}^{\prime\prime}
.
In summary, we have a closed manifold
\mathcal{M}
that satisfies the conditions of Subsection
4.1
with
\mathcal{S}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Let
G(t_{0})=G(R_{1},t_{0})
denote the metrics on
M
from Proposition
A.6
.
Consider the restriction of
G(R_{1},t_{0})
to
\overline{M}_{R+2}
where
R=12\sqrt{2}R_{1}
.
Then, in a neighborhood of
\partial\overline{M}_{R+2}
,
G(R_{1},t_{0})
is a cylindrical metric after pulling back by
\Psi
.
It follows that
G(R_{1},t_{0})
induces a smooth
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-invariant metric on the double
\mathcal{M}_{R+2}
which we still denote by
G(R_{1},t_{0})
.
Using that
\iota_{R+1}^{\prime\prime}:M_{R+1}\to M
restricts to the inclusion map on
M_{R}
, it now follows from Proposition
A.6
and Lemma
A.5
that, after pulling these metrics back by
F_{R+2}:\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{M}_{R+2}
(with
R=12\sqrt{2}R_{1}
), we obtain a family of metrics
G_{\mathbf{0}}(\Gamma_{0},t_{0})\doteqdot F^{*}_{48\sqrt{\Gamma_{0}}+2}G(\sqrt{8\Gamma_{0}},t_{0})
on
\mathcal{M}
satisfying Definition
4.2
for all
R_{1}=\sqrt{8\Gamma_{0}}\gg 1
sufficiently large depending on
n,M,\overline{g},f,N_{0},R_{0},\Psi
.
Note that since
N_{0},R_{0},\Psi
were fixed and depend on
n,M,\overline{g},f
,
this dependence reduces to all
\Gamma_{0}\gg 1
sufficiently large depending only on
n,M,\overline{g},f
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Harmonic Map Heat Flow

Throughout this section, we establish results for the harmonic map heat flow solutions appropriate to our setting.

The next result establishes short-time existence for the harmonic map heat flow.
It is a restatement of
[
BK22
, Proposition A.9]
in form more appropriate for our applications.

Proposition B.1

(Short-time existence of the harmonic map heat flow)
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{\prime}\ll 1
sufficiently small depending only on
n

the following holds:

If
(M^{n},g(t))_{t\in[t_{0},T]},(\overline{M}^{n},\overline{g}(t))_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
are smooth families of Riemannian manifolds and

F_{t_{0}}:M\to\overline{M}
is a smooth function such that

(1)

(M,g(t_{0}))
and
(\overline{M},\overline{g}(t_{0}))
are complete,

(2)

For all integers
0\leq m\leq 10
, there exist constants
C_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

|{}^{g(t)}\nabla^{m}Rm_{g(t)}|_{g(t)}(x)\leq C_{m}\quad\text{for all }(x,t)\in M\times[t_{0},T]\text{ and}

|{}^{\overline{g}(t)}\nabla^{m}Rm_{\overline{g}(t)}|_{\overline{g}(t)}(x)\leq C_{m}\quad\text{for all }(x,t)\in\overline{M}\times[t_{0},T],

(3)

(\overline{M},\overline{g}(t))_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
evolves by Ricci flow

\partial_{t}\overline{g}=-2Rc_{\overline{g}}\qquad\text{ on }\overline{M}\times(t_{0},T),

(4)

there exists a constant
C_{0}^{\prime}
such that

|\partial_{t}g+2Rc_{g(t)}|_{g(t)}\leq C_{0}^{\prime}\qquad\text{for all }(x,t)\in M\times[t_{0},T]

and

(5)

F_{t_{0}}:M\to\overline{M}
is a diffeomorphism with

|(F_{t_{0}}^{-1})^{*}g(t_{0})-\overline{g}(t_{0})|_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}\leq\epsilon,

then, for some
T_{F}
with
t_{0}<T_{F}\leq T
,
there exists a smooth solution
F:M\times[t_{0},T_{F}]\to\overline{M}

to the harmonic map heat flow

\partial_{t}F=\Delta_{g(t),\overline{g}(t)}F\qquad\text{ with initial condition }F(\cdot,t_{0})=F_{t_{0}}

such that

F(\cdot,t)\doteqdot F_{t}:M\to\overline{M}
is a diffeomorphism for all
t\in[t_{0},T_{F}]

and

|(F_{t}^{-1})^{*}g(t)-\overline{g}(t)|_{\overline{g}(t)}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\qquad\text{on }\overline{M}\times[t_{0},T_{F}].

Moreover,

T_{F}\in(t_{0},T]
can be chosen such that

T_{F}\geq\min\{t_{0}+\tau,T\}

where
\tau=\tau(n,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},C_{0},C_{0}^{\prime})>0
is a positive constant depending only on
n,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},C_{0},C_{0}^{\prime}

and such that

\sup_{\overline{M}}|(F_{T_{F}}^{-1})^{*}g(T_{F})-\overline{g}(T_{F})|_{\overline{g}(T_{F})}<\epsilon^{\prime}\quad\implies\quad T_{F}=T.

Proof.

This proposition is a direct consequence of
[
BK22
, Proposition A.9]
with

C=C_{0}
,

C^{\prime}=\max_{0\leq m\leq 10}C_{m}T^{m/2}
,

\delta=C_{0}^{\prime}
,

\eta_{0}=\epsilon
,

\eta_{1}=\epsilon^{\prime}
,

\eta^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\overline{M}}|(F_{T_{F}}^{-1})^{*}g(T_{F})-\overline{g}(T_{F})|_{\overline{g}(T_{F})}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{\prime}
,

\overline{\chi}=F_{t_{0}}
,

\chi_{t}=F_{t_{0}+t}
,

g^{\prime}(t)=g(t_{0}+t)
,

g(t)=\overline{g}(t_{0}+t)
, and

T=T-t_{0}
.
∎

Remark B.2
.

In practice,
\overline{g}(t)
will be the soliton solution
\overline{g}(t)=(1-t)\varphi_{t}^{*}\overline{g}
,

g(t)
will be
\acute{G}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
,
and
F=\tilde{\Phi}
.

Lemma B.3

(Drift Control)
.

Let
0\leq t_{0}<T\leq 1
.
Let
(M^{n},g(t))_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
and
(\overline{M}^{n},\overline{g}(t))_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
be smooth families of complete Riemannian manifolds and let
(F_{t}:M\to\overline{M})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
be a smooth family of diffeomorphisms such that

\partial_{t}\overline{g}=-2\overline{Rc}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{t}F_{t}=\Delta_{g,\overline{g}}F_{t}.

Let

h\doteqdot(F_{t}^{-1})^{*}g(t)-\overline{g}(t).

Assume that

\displaystyle\sup_{\overline{M}\times[t_{0},T]}|\overline{Rm}|_{\overline{g}}\leq\overline{\mathcal{K}}<\infty,

\displaystyle\sup_{(x,t)\in\overline{M}\times[t_{0},T]}|h|_{\overline{g}}(x,t)\leq\epsilon,\text{ and}

\displaystyle\sup_{(x,t)\in\overline{M}\times[t_{0},T]}\sqrt{1-t}|\overline{\nabla}h|_{\overline{g}}(x,t)\leq\epsilon.

If
0<\epsilon\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending only on
n
,
then there exists
C
depending only on
n
and
\overline{\mathcal{K}}
such that

d_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(F_{t}(x),F_{t_{0}}(x))\leq C\epsilon\left(\sqrt{1-t_{0}}-\sqrt{1-t}\right)\leq C\epsilon\sqrt{1-t_{0}}

for all
(x,t)\in M\times[t_{0},T].

Proof.

For any
\overline{x}\in\overline{M}
, observe

\partial_{t}F_{t}\circ F_{t}^{-1}(\overline{x})=-B_{\overline{g}}(\overline{g}+h)(\overline{x})

where
B_{\overline{g}}(\overline{g}+h)
is defined by (
4.11
) in Lemma
4.15
.
If
0<\epsilon\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending only on
n
, then

|\partial_{t}F_{t}\circ F_{t}^{-1}|_{\overline{g}}(\overline{x})=|B_{\overline{g}}(\overline{g}+h)|_{\overline{g}}(\overline{x})\lesssim_{n}|\overline{\nabla}h|_{\overline{g}}(\overline{x})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{1-t}}.

Since the
F_{t}:M\to\overline{M}
are diffeomorphisms,

|\partial_{t}F_{t}(x)|_{\overline{g}}\lesssim_{n}\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{1-t}}\qquad\text{for all }x\in M.

It follows that

\displaystyle d_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(F_{t}(x),F_{t_{0}}(x))

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\frac{d}{ds}d_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(F_{s}(x),F_{t_{0}}(x))ds

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\langle\nabla d,\partial_{s}F_{s}\rangle_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}ds

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left|\partial_{s}F_{s}\right|_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}ds.

Since the Ricci flow solution
\overline{g}(t)
has uniformly bounded curvature for
t\in[t_{0},T]\subset[0,1]
,
the metrics
\overline{g}(t)
are comparable for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
with

\overline{g}(t^{\prime})\lesssim_{n,\overline{K}}\overline{g}(t)\lesssim_{n,\overline{K}}\overline{g}(t^{\prime})\qquad\text{for all }t,t^{\prime}\in[t_{0},T]

(see for example
[
Ham95
, proof of Theorem 8.1]
).
Hence,

\displaystyle d_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(F_{t}(x),F_{t_{0}}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left|\partial_{s}F_{s}\right|_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}ds

\displaystyle\lesssim_{n,\overline{\mathcal{K}}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left|\partial_{s}F_{s}\right|_{\overline{g}(s)}ds

\displaystyle\lesssim_{n}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{1-s}}ds

\displaystyle=\epsilon\left(2\sqrt{1-t_{0}}-2\sqrt{1-t}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon\sqrt{1-t_{0}}.

∎

Remark B.4
.

The choice to use the distance function at time
t_{0}
in the above Lemma
B.3
was arbitrary.
In fact, since
\overline{Rm}
is uniformly bounded and
0\leq T-t_{0}\leq 1
in Lemma
B.3
,
the distance functions are all comparable, that is

d_{\overline{g}(t)}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\sim_{n,\overline{\mathcal{K}}}d_{\overline{g}(t^{\prime})}(\overline{x},\overline{y})\qquad\text{for all }\overline{x},\overline{y}\in\overline{M}\text{ and }t,t^{\prime}\in[t_{0},T],

by
[
Ham95
, Theorem 17.1]
.

Unfortunately, the drift control estimates of the previous lemma do not suffice for some of our purposes, and we need the following drift estimate where curvature bounds are assumed only on a subset
\Omega^{\prime}
of the manifold
\overline{M}
.

Lemma B.5

(Local Drift Control)
.

Let
0\leq t_{0}<T\leq 1
.
Let
(M^{n},g(t))_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
and
(\overline{M}^{n},\overline{g}(t))_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
be smooth families of complete Riemannian manifolds and let
(F_{t}:M\to\overline{M})_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
be a smooth family of diffeomorphisms such that

\partial_{t}\overline{g}=-2\overline{Rc}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{t}F_{t}=\Delta_{g,\overline{g}}F_{t}.

Let

h\doteqdot(F_{t}^{-1})^{*}g(t)-\overline{g}(t)

and
\Omega\Subset\Omega^{\prime}\subset\overline{M}
.
Assume that

\displaystyle\sup_{\Omega^{\prime}\times[t_{0},T]}|\overline{Rm}|_{\overline{g}}\leq\overline{\mathcal{K}}<\infty,

\displaystyle\sup_{(x,t)\in\overline{M}\times[t_{0},T]}|h|_{\overline{g}}(x,t)\leq\epsilon,\text{ and}

\displaystyle\sup_{(x,t)\in\overline{M}\times[t_{0},T]}\sqrt{1-t}|\overline{\nabla}h|_{\overline{g}}(x,t)\leq\epsilon.

If
0<\epsilon\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending only on
n

and
0<1-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,\overline{\mathcal{K}},\dist_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(\Omega,\overline{M}\setminus\Omega^{\prime})
,
then

F_{t_{0}}(x)\in\Omega\implies F_{t}(x)\in\Omega^{\prime}\text{ for all }t\in[t_{0},T].

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists
x\in M
and
t\in[t_{0},T]
such that

F_{t_{0}}(x)\in\Omega
and
F_{t}(x)\notin\Omega^{\prime}
.
Then we can define

t_{*}\doteqdot\inf\{t\in[t_{0},T]:F_{t}(x)\notin\Omega^{\prime}\}.

It follows that

d_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(F_{t_{0}}(x),F_{t_{*}}(x))\geq d_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(\Omega,\overline{M}\setminus\Omega^{\prime})>0.

By the same argument as in the beginning of the proof of Lemma
B.3
,

|\partial_{t}F_{t}|_{\overline{g}}(F_{t}(x))\lesssim_{n}\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{1-t}}

for all
t\in[t_{0},T]
if
0<\epsilon\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n
.
Hence,

\displaystyle d_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(F_{t_{*}}(x),F_{t_{0}}(x))

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{*}}\frac{d}{ds}d_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(F_{s}(x),F_{t_{0}}(x))ds

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{*}}\langle\nabla d,\partial_{s}F_{s}(x)\rangle_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}ds

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{*}}\left|\partial_{s}F_{s}\right|_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(F_{s}(x))ds.

By definition of
t_{*}
,
F_{s}(x)\in\Omega^{\prime}
for all
s\in[t_{0},t_{*})
.
Since the Ricci flow solution
\overline{g}(t)
has uniformly bounded curvature on
\Omega^{\prime}\times[t_{0},T]\subset\Omega^{\prime}\times[0,1]
,
the metrics
\overline{g}(t)
at
F_{t}(x)\in\overline{M}
are comparable for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{*}]
with

\overline{g}(t^{\prime})\lesssim_{n,\overline{\mathcal{K}}}\overline{g}(t)\lesssim_{n,\overline{\mathcal{K}}}\overline{g}(t^{\prime})\qquad\text{at }F_{t}(x)\in\overline{M},\text{ for all }t,t^{\prime}\in[t_{0},t_{*}]

(see for example
[
Ham95
, proof of Theorem 8.1]
).
Hence,

\displaystyle d_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(F_{t_{*}}(x),F_{t_{0}}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{*}}\left|\partial_{s}F_{s}\right|_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(F_{s}(x))ds

\displaystyle\lesssim_{n,\overline{\mathcal{K}}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{*}}\left|\partial_{s}F_{s}\right|_{\overline{g}(s)}(F_{s}(x))ds

\displaystyle\lesssim_{n}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{1-s}}ds

\displaystyle=\epsilon\left(2\sqrt{1-t_{0}}-2\sqrt{1-t_{*}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon\sqrt{1-t_{0}}.

In particular, if
\epsilon<1
and
0<1-t_{0}\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending on
n,\overline{\mathcal{K}},d_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(\Omega,\overline{M}\setminus\Omega^{\prime})
, then

d_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(F_{t_{*}}(x),F_{t_{0}}(x))<d_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(\Omega,\overline{M}\setminus\Omega^{\prime})\leq d_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(F_{t_{*}}(x),F_{t_{0}}(x)),

a contradiction.
∎

Proposition B.6

(Uniqueness of the Harmonic Map Heat Flow Solution)
.

Let
0\leq t_{0}<T\leq 1
.
Let
(M^{n},g(t))_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
and
(\overline{M}^{n},\overline{g}(t))_{t\in[t_{0},T]}
be smooth families of complete Riemannian manifolds such that
\overline{g}(t)
evolves by Ricci flow

\partial_{t}\overline{g}=-2\overline{Rc}.

Let
F_{1},F_{2}:M\times[t_{0},T]\to\overline{M}
be smooth functions such that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}F_{1}&=\Delta_{g,\overline{g}}F_{1}&&\text{on }M\times(t_{0},T),\\
\partial_{t}F_{2}&=\Delta_{g,\overline{g}}F_{2}&&\text{on }M\times(t_{0},T),\\
F_{1}(x,t_{0})&=F_{2}(x,t_{0})&&\text{for all }x\in M,\text{ and}\\
\end{aligned}

\displaystyle F_{1}(\cdot,t),F_{2}(\cdot,t):M\to\overline{M}\text{ are diffeomorphisms for all }t\in[t_{0},T].

Let

h_{1}\doteqdot(F_{1}(\cdot,t)^{-1})^{*}g-\overline{g}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad h_{2}\doteqdot(F_{2}(\cdot,t)^{-1})^{*}g-\overline{g}.

Assume that

\displaystyle\sup_{\overline{M}\times[t_{0},T]}|\overline{Rm}|_{\overline{g}}\leq\overline{\mathcal{K}}<\infty,\qquad\inf_{\overline{M}\times[t_{0},T]}\inj(\overline{M},\overline{g}(t))\geq i_{0}>0,

\displaystyle\sup_{\overline{M}\times[t_{0},T]}\left(|h_{1}|_{\overline{g}}(x,t)+|h_{2}|_{\overline{g}}(x,t)\right)\leq\epsilon,\text{ and}

\displaystyle\sup_{\overline{M}\times[t_{0},T]}\sqrt{1-t}\left(|\overline{\nabla}h_{1}|_{\overline{g}}(x,t)+|\overline{\nabla}h_{2}|_{\overline{g}}(x,t)\right)\leq\epsilon.

If
0<\epsilon\ll 1
is sufficiently small depending only on
n
,
then
F_{1}(x,t)=F_{2}(x,t)
for all
(x,t)\in M\times[t_{0},T]
.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that

t_{0}^{\prime}\doteqdot\max\{t^{\prime}\in[t_{0},T]:F_{1}(\cdot,t)=F_{2}(\cdot,t)\text{ for all }t\in[t_{0},t^{\prime}]\}<T.

Consider the function
u:M\times[t_{0}^{\prime},T]\to\mathbb{R}
given by the squared distance of
F_{1}
to
F_{2}
, that is,

u(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}d_{\overline{g}(t)}^{2}(F_{1}(x,t),F_{2}(x,t)).

Note
u(x,t_{0}^{\prime})=0
for all
x\in M
.

The uniform injectivity radius lower bound implies that there exists a sufficiently small neighborhood
U=B_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(D,r)
of the diagonal
D\subset\overline{M}\times\overline{M}
where the squared distance function
(\overline{x},\overline{y},t)\mapsto d_{\overline{g}(t)}^{2}(\overline{x},\overline{y})
is twice differentiable.
Additionally, the uniform curvature bounds

\sup_{\overline{M}\times[t_{0},T]}|\overline{Rm}|\leq\overline{\mathcal{K}}

yield estimates on the Hessian
\nabla^{2}d={}^{\overline{M}\times\overline{M}}\nabla^{2}d

which in particular imply
that the Hessian
\nabla^{2}d
is positive semidefinite on
U=B_{\overline{g}(t_{0})}(D,r)\subset\overline{M}\times\overline{M}
after possibly taking
r
smaller.
By the drift control estimate Lemma
B.3
, there exists
0<\tau\ll 1
sufficiently small depending on
n,\overline{\mathcal{K}},i_{0},\epsilon

such that, for all
t\in[t_{0}^{\prime},t_{0}^{\prime}+\tau]\subset[t_{0},T]
,
the image of
x\mapsto(F_{1}(x,t),F_{2}(x,t))
stays within this neighborhood
U
.

We can now compute and estimate the evolution equation for
u
on
M\times(t_{0}^{\prime},t_{0}^{\prime}+\tau)
in terms of the induced connections:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u={}

\displaystyle d_{\overline{g}}(F_{1},F_{2})\cdot\left[(\partial_{t}d_{\overline{g}(t)})|_{(F_{1},F_{2})}+(\nabla d)(\partial_{t}F_{1}\oplus\partial_{t}F_{2})\right]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle d_{\overline{g}}(F_{1},F_{2})\cdot\left[(\partial_{t}d_{\overline{g}(t)})|_{(F_{1},F_{2})}+(\nabla d)(\Delta_{g,\overline{g}}F_{1}\oplus\Delta_{g,\overline{g}}F_{2})\right],

\displaystyle\nabla_{j}u={}

\displaystyle d_{\overline{g}}(F_{1},F_{2})\cdot\left[(\nabla d)|_{(F_{1},F_{2})}(\nabla_{j}F_{1}\oplus\nabla_{j}F_{2})\right],\text{ and}

\displaystyle\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}u={}

\displaystyle\left[(\nabla d)(\nabla_{i}F_{1}\oplus\nabla_{i}F_{2})\right]\left[(\nabla d)(\nabla_{j}F_{1}\oplus\nabla_{j}F_{2})\right]

\displaystyle+d_{\overline{g}}(F_{1},F_{2})\cdot\left[(\nabla d)(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}F_{1}\oplus\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}F_{2})\right]

\displaystyle+d_{\overline{g}}(F_{1},F_{2})\cdot\left[(\nabla^{2}d)(\nabla_{i}F_{1}\oplus\nabla_{i}F_{2},\nabla_{j}F_{1}\oplus\nabla_{j}F_{2})\right].

It follows that

(B.1)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}-\Delta_{g})u={}&d_{\overline{g}}(F_{1},F_{2})\cdot(\partial_{t}d_{\overline{g}(t)})|_{(F_{1},F_{2})}-|(\nabla d)(\nabla F_{1}\oplus\nabla F_{2})|_{g}^{2}\\
&-d_{\overline{g}}(F_{1},F_{2})\cdot g^{ij}\left[(\nabla^{2}d)(\nabla_{i}F_{1}\oplus\nabla_{i}F_{2},\nabla_{j}F_{1}\oplus\nabla_{j}F_{2})\right].\end{aligned}

It follows from
[
Ham95
, Lemma 17.3]
that

d_{\overline{g}}(F_{1},F_{2})\cdot(\partial_{t}d_{\overline{g}(t)})(F_{1},F_{2})\leq\|\overline{Rc}\|_{C^{0}(\overline{M})}d_{\overline{g}}(F_{1},F_{2})^{2}\leq C_{n}\overline{\mathcal{K}}u.

Writing the last term in the evolution equation (
B.1
) in terms of a
g
-orthonormal basis
\{e_{i}\}
,
the estimate

\displaystyle-d_{\overline{g}}(F_{1},F_{2})\cdot g^{ij}\left[(\nabla^{2}d)(\nabla_{i}F_{1}\oplus\nabla_{i}F_{2},\nabla_{j}F_{1}\oplus\nabla_{j}F_{2})\right]

\displaystyle={}
